raising credit-rating standards and changing how credit-rating agencies
are compensated, forbidding
propr<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA290.w.1.2.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>ietary trading by banks (that is, trading of
their<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA291.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_para.314.1.0.box.153.310.904.485.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA291"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA291.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA291.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> equity capital, which puts that capital at risk),
adjusting reserve requirements to take more realistic account of the
riskiness of banks' capital structures, requiring greater disclosure by
hedge funds and private equity funds, requiring that credit-default
swaps be traded on exchanges and fully collateralized, and even
resurrecting usury laws. Obviously this is just a partial list.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA291.w.1.0.0.1" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA291.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA291" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0017.xml_block.314.1.0.box.153.310.906.1603.q.70"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA291.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

But this is not the time either to reorganize or to reregulate the
financial industry. The case against reorganization is the clearer:
experience, as with the Department of Homeland Security, teaches that a
major federal reorganization (not to mention a reorganization that would
encompass both state and foreign regulation of financial intermediation
as well) takes years to gel, and during those years of growing pains the
efficiency with which the mission entrusted to the reorganized entity
will be performed will be lower than it was in the pre-reorganization
regime. And this is apa<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA291.w.1.1.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>rt from the fact that in the present instance the
same small knot of senior economic officials that would design and
supervise the reorganization have their hands full dealing with an
economic emergency.

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA291.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA291.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>The
case against trying to reregulate financial intermediation at this time
is a bit subtler and
re<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA292.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_para.315.1.0.box.152.321.910.1612.q.70"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA292"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA292.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA292.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span>quires me to distinguish between two senses of
"regulation." In one sense it refers to the regulatory *framework —* the
laws that establish the powers and limits of the regulatory body. In
another sense it refers to the regulatory *regime* — the actual
administration of the regulatory scheme, which involves the exercise by
officials of discretion within the limits set by the legal framework.
"Deregulation" usually refers to changing the regulatory framework in
the direction of relaxing controls over the regulated firms in order to
make the market freer and more competitive. In that sense the
der<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA292.w.1.0.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>egulation movement in the financial industry was
largely completed in the 1990s. But because deregulation of the
regulatory framework is usually and in this instance incomplete,
"deregulation" can also refer to changes in the regulatory regime, and
in that sense deregulation continued making long strides, which turned
out to be missteps, in the 2000s. Alan Greenspan, exercising the vast
discretion reposed by Congress in the Federal Reserve, decided to push
interest rates down and keep them down. He and his successor also
decided to use a light touch in regulating banking practices, over
which<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA292.w.1.0.0.2"
shape="rect"></span> the Federal Reserve also has vast discretionary
control. Notably the Fed can increase a bank's reserve
re</span><span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA293.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_para.316.1.0.box.152.312.908.423.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA293"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA293.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA293.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span>quirements (and thus constrain its ability to lend)
if it determines that the bank's capital is too risky. The Bush
Administration took a decidedly handsoff attitude toward the financial
sector, typified by the passivity of the Securities and Exchange
Commission under Cox's chairmanship. A regime of laxity inhabited a
rickety framework.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA293.w.1.0.0.1" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA293.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA292" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0017.xml_block.315.1.0.box.152.321.910.1612.q.70"
class="gtxt_body">

</div>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA293" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0017.xml_block.316.1.0.box.151.312.909.1604.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA293.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

All this will now change, just as the civil rights regime of the Clinton
Administration, which emphasized race and gender discrimination, gave
way to the Bush Administration's civil rights regime, which emphasized
religious discrimination. But until the new, bound-to-be activist
regulatory regime (in which Bernanke, though a holdover, is likely to
participate willingly) has had a chance to show what it can do within
the existing regulatory framework, it is premature to alter the
framework.

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA293.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA293.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>And
for the further reason that while reregulation is likely to be
administratively less complex than reorganization (just count the
federal and state agencies that would have to be included in one way or
another in the reorganization for it to be maximally effective), it is
more challenging intellectually. There is first the extraordinary
diversity and complexity of modern financial intermediation. Commercial
banks conduct little<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA294.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_para.317.1.0.box.153.294.906.486.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA294"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA294.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA294.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> more than half the financial intermediation in the
American economy. If they are forced to be safe, their competitors will
eat them alive. But can hedge funds, private equity funds, investment
banks, and all the other nonbank banks be placed under the identical
regulatory regime as commercial banks? If not, won't the differences
distort competition?</span>

</div>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA294" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0017.xml_block.317.1.0.box.153.294.910.1605.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA294.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA294.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>It is
easier to deregulate an industry than to reregulate it. Deregulation has
a built-in momentum: allow an unregulated firm to compete with a
regulated one and the regulated firm will have a convincing case that it
must be deregulated so that it can compete. One ends with a competitive
industry. The competitive financial-intermediation industry that
deregulation has created is complex and varied. The industry cannot
readily be made homogeneous, yet its heterogeneity greatly magnifies the
difficulty of designing and implementing a comprehensive system of
regulation.

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA294.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA294.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>We
could, in principle anyway, restore the regulatory framework of
commercial banking that existed in the 1960s. Bank capital would consist
mainly of zero-interest demand deposits and federal securities and would
be used mainly to make short-term commercial loans. But we know
that<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA295.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_para.318.1.0.box.152.309.905.862.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA295"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA295.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA295.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> this model of banking would not be viable if other
financial intermediaries were permitted, as they are today, to offer
close substitutes for bank products. Does this mean, however, that money
market funds, hedge funds, and all the other nonbank banks must be
placed under the same regulatory controls as commercial banks? Should
they for example be required to have reserves? To pay zero interest to
the lenders of their capital? If the answer to these questions is yes,
that is the end of hedge funds, of money market funds, etc. If the
answer is no, it is unclear how much
reregulatio<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA295.w.1.0.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>n of commercial banks is possible. If there is
another answer, it will take much thought to work out.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA295.w.1.0.0.2" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA295.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA295" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0017.xml_block.318.1.0.box.152.309.909.1614.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA295.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

There is a further and very serious complication. As far as I know, no
one has a clear sense of the social value of our deregulated financial
industry, with its free-wheeling banks and hedge funds and private
equity funds and all the rest. The profits were, until the financial
crisis of last fall, enormous, but undoubtedly contained a large amount
of economic rent. (This is related to the point I made in chapter 6
about the possible overcompensation of physics Ph.D.'s who go to work
for Wall Street.) When A sells B stock worth \$10 a share, and the next
day it is worth \$15, the
<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA295.w.1.1.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>country is
not<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA296.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_para.319.1.0.box.155.297.902.608.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA296"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA296.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA296.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> \$5 richer, though B is. Obviously a stock exchange
and a credit system are enormous public goods (as we are learning anew
in this depression), but the value added by the vast increases in recent
years in the amount of speculative trading is unclear. Until we get a
clear idea of what it is, we do not know what the costs would be of
adopting a 1960s-style model of the financial system, or, more
realistically, taking some large steps in that direction. Reregulation,
like reorganization, should wait.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA296" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0017.xml_block.319.1.0.box.155.297.905.1610.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA296.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA296.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>Short
of comprehensive reregulation, a few piecemeal reforms may be feasible
and helpful. But I am afraid that the list that follows is pretty small
beer. Maybe, for example, the government should stop talking up
homeownership. True, there is an argument and some evidence that
homeownership creates external benefits because owners care more about
the appearance of their house and their neighborhood than an absentee
landlord does, and the entire neighborhood benefits. But since most
owned homes as distinct from rented ones are detached houses located in
suburbs, homeownership
promot<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA296.w.1.1.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>es suburban sprawl, traffic congestion, and carbon
emissions. The exemption from federal income tax of interest on
residential mortgages (including home equity
loans)<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA297.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_para.320.1.0.box.155.339.902.299.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA297"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA297.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA297.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> and of real estate taxes is a heavy subsidy for
homeownership. It is subsidy enough, or more than enough. There is no
good argument for the government's propagandizing for homeownership and
pushing banks to make risky mortgage loans.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA297.w.1.0.0.1" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA297.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA297" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0017.xml_block.320.1.0.box.155.339.904.1613.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA297.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

I mentioned that among the bubble factors were the level and structure
of executive compensation. Efforts to place legal limits on compensation
are bound to fail, or to be defeated by loopholes, or to cause
distortions in the executive labor market and in corporate behavior. An
example would be limiting bonuses to financial executives. The fact that
the banking industry paid billions of dollars in bonuses in 2008 is
arousing the ignorant indignation of the press and the calculated
indignation of politicians. As much as half the income of such
executives consists of bonuses, and
thi<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA297.w.1.1.0.1" shape="rect"></span>s
enables a better matching of income to performance than flat salaries
do. So to have eliminated all bonuses would have amounted to a 50
percent salary cut for many financial executives. The bonuses were
smaller than in previous years, so there was a salary cut, and that was
appropriate; it is unclear whether a greater cut would have been
optimal.

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA297.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA297.w.1.2.0"
shape="rect"></span>Measures short of capping compensation may be worth
considering, though I cannot work up a
great<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA298.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_para.321.1.0.box.152.294.905.923.q.70"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA298"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA298.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA298.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> deal of enthusiasm for them. Initially, at least,
they should be limited to executives of banks and other financial
intermediaries, as that is where executivecompensation practices menace
the entire economy. The effect would be to drive some financial
executives (and prospective financial executives) into other fields, but
that would not be a bad thing, as I have explained. The biggest problem
is that to be effective in reducing the likelihood of catastrophic bank
failures, the limits on compensation could not be confined to CEOs and
other senior officials. The actual trading
deci<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA298.w.1.0.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>sions are made lower down in the corporate
hierarchy. It is hard to see how the government could fix a salary
schedule for the entire professional staff of a bank.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA298" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0017.xml_block.321.1.0.box.152.294.909.1614.q.70"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA298.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA298.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>A
simple, and I imagine an inevitable, reform would be to require
corporations, both those that are publicly held (that is, their stock is
traded on an exchange and the ownership of the stock is widely
dispersed) and those that are privately held (otherwise the requirement
will drive many publicly held firms private), to disclose the *full*
compensation of all senior executives. This would include pension
entitlements discounted to present value, health benefits, severance
pay, private use of corporate facilities including airplanes,
limousines, and apart<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA299.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_para.322.1.0.box.156.315.902.169.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA299"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA299.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA299.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span>ments, club memberships paid for by the corporation,
and all other perquisites, monetized where possible and subject to
public audit.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA299.w.1.0.0.1" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA299.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA299" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0017.xml_block.322.1.0.box.156.315.908.1610.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA299.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

A second fairly modest reform would be to require that a substantial
share of the compensation of financial executives be backloadcd and tied
to the corporation's future performance. *For* example, a corporation
might be forbidden to provide severance pay to its CEO (though it could
pay him a signing bonus, since that wouldn't give him an incentive to
impart greater risk to the corporation) and required to pay him a
specified percentage of his compensation in the form of restricted stock
in the corporation —stock that he could not sell (or redeem, in the case
of a privately held corporation)
<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA299.w.1.1.0.1" shape="rect"></span>for
a specified number of years. Such a reform would combat the dangerous
incentive of highly compensated CEOs to maximize short-term corporate
profits and, to that end, to take excessive risks with the corporation's
assets.

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA299.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA299.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>The
problem (it is the same as the problem of stock options) is that the
performance of an individual employee in a large corporation, even if he
is the CEO, is unlikely to have a measurable effect on the value of the
corporation's stock. An alternative that is receiving increasing
attention, probably<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA300.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_para.323.1.0.box.153.294.904.359.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA300"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA300.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA300.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> rightly so, is "claw back": part of the employee's
bonus is placed in an account, and if he has a bad year the account is
reduced. This is an effective method of fitting pay to performance if
the employee is solely or primarily responsible for specific
transactions involving measurable profits or losses.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA300" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0017.xml_block.323.1.0.box.153.294.909.1608.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA300.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

Consideration should perhaps be given to increasing the marginal income
tax rate of persons who have very high incomes, in order to reduce their
appetite for risk-taking. Such incomes typically contain a good deal of
economic rent. Think of the boxing champion who makes
mi<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA300.w.1.0.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>llions but whose next best job would be as a bouncer
in a strip joint, paid the minimum wage. Taxing economic rents is
efficient because it has, by definition (and in my example), minimal
substitution effects. It will not deflect the taxpayer to a different
occupation just because it taxes the income available to him only in his
present one. An increase in income tax cannot very well be limited to
financial executives—it would be like making them wear dunce caps —but I
do not think there is a compelling objection to raising the marginal tax
rate on *all* high earners. Taxes will have to be
raised<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA300.w.1.0.0.2"
shape="rect"></span> at some point in order to finance the
anti-depression programs, and income taxes are more efficient taxes
than, sav,<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA301.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_para.324.1.0.box.155.306.904.289.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA301"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA301.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA301.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> corporate taxes. The refusal or inability of the
Bush Administration either to raise taxes or to reduce spending caused a
dangerous increase in the national debt. This is not the time to raise
taxes, but we are an undertaxed nation.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA301.w.1.0.0.1" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA301.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA301" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0017.xml_block.324.1.0.box.152.306.910.1616.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA301.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

The suggested measures have drawbacks. For example, in practice
progressive taxation abounds with loopholes and distorts the allocation
of resources, and unless it took the form of an excessprofits tax, which
would be unmanageable, it would hit indiscriminately incomes that did
and incomes that did not contain substantial economic rents. And forcing
greater transparency on financial corporations by requiring them to
disclose publicly the full value of their senior executives'
compensation might turn out to be a case of closing the barn door after
the horses have escaped. For in
<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA301.w.1.1.0.1" shape="rect"></span>the
wake of the financial crisis the debate over CEO compensation has become
so heated that efforts to conceal such compensation are likely to fail.
Maybe the attainment of transparency can be left to the market, aided by
aggressive financial media.

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA301.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA301.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>About
all that can be said for the measures that I have suggested may warrant
consideration now, while the depression is ongoing, is that they
skirt<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA302.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_para.325.1.0.box.155.297.901.161.q.70"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA302"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA302.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA302.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> the profound and intractable issues involved in
deciding whether to reregulate financial intermediation.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA302" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0017.xml_block.325.1.0.box.155.297.907.1609.q.70"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA302.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA302.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>A
further point may be worth noting. The existing regulatory protections
of investors and consumers, including corporate-governance regulations
designed to align executives' incentives with overall economic welfare,
and the existing regulation of mortgages and other forms of credit,
should not be thought of as merely investor-protection or
consumer-protection measures, akin to laws against fraud. They are also
macroeconomic tools, like the Federal Reserve's power to raise interest
rates —a power that failed to prevent the crisis of 2008.

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA302.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA302.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>But
the point I particularly wish to emphasize is that the President and his
advisers have their hands full dealing with the depression and with a
series of urgent foreign and security policy issues. It is a temptation,
but would I think be a mistake, for the new Administration to try to
emulate Franklin Roosevelt's astonishing first hundred days. The United
States fortunately is in less desperate straits today and American
government and the American economy, and specifically the American
banking system, are all immensely more complex than they were in 1933.
It would take year<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA302.w.1.2.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>s for a new
system<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA303.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_para.326.1.0.box.155.317.902.358.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA303"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA303.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA303.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> of financial regulation administered by a newly
consolidated regulatory administration to shake down. The six or eight
government responses to the depression that are or soon will be under
way are enough for now. Let the comprehensive structural solution await
calmer days.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA303.w.1.0.0.1" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA303.w.1.0.0.2" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0017.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA303" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0017.xml_block.326.1.0.box.155.317.902.358.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

</div>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0017.xml_GBS.PA303.w.1.0.0.3" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<span id="content-0018.xml"></span>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA304"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA304.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA304" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0018.xml_block.327.0.0.box.307.402.600.186.q.70"
class="gtxt_body" style="margin-bottom: 1.5em;">

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA304.w.0.0.0" shape="rect"></span>

11

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA304.w.0.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA304.w.0.1.0" shape="rect"></span>The
Future of Conservatism

</div>

<div id="content-0018.xml_block.327.1.0.box.153.803.906.1114.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA304.w.1.0.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span class="smallcaps"><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA304.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span>The </span><span class="smallcaps">Defeat </span>of
the Republican Party in the November 2008 election —a defeat the
magnitude of which probably owed a lot to the financial crisis and the
inept response to it of the McCain campaign—is widely thought to have
marked the eclipse of conservatism in the United States. The Bush
Administration's indecisive response to the gathering depression in its
last few months in power invites comparison to Hoover's ineffectual
efforts in his lame-duck phase to check the deepening depression.
Hoover's failure ushered in twenty years of Democratic Party dominance.
The Bush Administration's response
t<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA304.w.1.0.0.1" shape="rect"></span>o
the crisis, although belated, was far more vigorous than Hoover's (and
Hoover had almost three and a half years to respond to his economic
crisis). But it will get little credit for that because of the
impression that President Bush gave, rightly or wrongly, of being
AWOL<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA305.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_para.328.1.0.box.155.311.905.285.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA305"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA305.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA305.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> while the economy was melting beneath his feet. And
his Administration's responses to the crisis were inept, despite all
that had been learned since Hoover's time about depressions—but perhaps
not that much had been learned!</span>

</div>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA305.w.1.0.0.1" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA305.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA305" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0018.xml_block.328.1.0.box.152.311.908.1611.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA305.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

We should distinguish between the Republican Party and conservatism.
Political parties in a twoparty system are opportunistic coalitions and
as a result lack ideological homogeneity, especially in a culturally
heterogeneous nation such as the United States. Apart from the many
Republicans and Democrats who vote for a party out of habit, nostalgia,
or family tradition, or out of attachment to a particular issue — or
even out of a personal liking or loathing for the other people who vote
for a party—there are ideological voters. In the Republican Party they
fall into three<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA305.w.1.1.0.1"
shape="rect"></span> main groups: believers in (1) free markets, low
taxes, and small government—economic conservatives; (2) believers in
tough criminal laws and a strong foreign policy— I'll call them security
conservatives; and (3) social (mainly religious) conservatives, who are
hostile to abortion, gay marriage, pornography, gun control, and a clean
separation of church and state. The security and social conservatives
converge on hostility to illegal immigrants. The economic and
secu<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA306.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_para.329.1.0.box.154.295.906.347.q.70"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA306"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA306.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA306.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span>rity conservatives are in some tension because a
national-security state requires a big government and therefore high
taxes, and the economic conservatives are in tension with the social
conservatives because the former are libertarian and the latter are
interventionist.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA306" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0018.xml_block.329.1.0.box.154.295.908.1615.q.70"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA306.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA306.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>All
three groups have been damaged by recent events and are moving apart
from each other because of the blows that the others have received. The
depression has hit economic libertarians in their solar plexus, because
it is largely a consequence not of the government's overregulating the
economy and by doing so fettering free enterprise, but rather of innate
limitations of the free market— limitations rooted in individuals'
incentives, in irresponsible monetary policy adopted and executed by
conservative officials inspired by conservative economists who thought
that easy money was no
problem<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA306.w.1.1.0.1"
shape="rect"></span> if it did not lead to serious inflation, and in
excessive, ideologically motivated deregulation of banking and finance
compounded by lax enforcement of the remaining regulations. The
depression and the Bush Administration's unsteady response to it, in
combination with its lavish predepression deficit spending, have greatly
damaged the Republicans' reputation for fiscal rectitude.

</div>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA307" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0018.xml_block.330.1.0.box.154.314.906.1612.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA307.w.1.0.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA307"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA307.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA307.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span>Believers in a strong foreign policy have been
embarrassed by the protracted, expensive, and distracting war in Iraq—a
war that has sown discord between the United States and its allies,
increased recruitment of Islamic terrorists, strengthened Iran, and
strengthened the Taliban and Al-Qaeda in Afghanistan and Pakistan.
Security conservatives have also been embarrassed by the Bush
Administration's lack of success in meeting challenges to U.S. security
posed by Iran, North Korea, Afghanistan, Pakistan, and the Arab-Israeli
conflict. Social conservatives have been damaged by the stridency of
so<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA307.w.1.0.0.1" shape="rect"></span>me
of their most prominent advocates, who sometimes give the impression of
being meanspirited, out-of-touch, know-nothing deniers of science—
notably of evolution, manmade climate change, sexual orientation, and
even brain death (the Terri Schiavo affair).

<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA307.w.1.0.0.2"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA307.w.1.1.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA307.w.1.1.0"
shape="rect"></span>

These wounds, along with a sense of the Bush Administration's lack of
managerial competence, have weakened Republican claims to be dependable
trustees of the economy, of the national security, and of the moral life
of the nation. The efficiency gap between the competing presidential
campaigns reinforced the appearance of a competence gap between the
parties. During the
cam<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA308.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_para.331.1.0.box.154.299.906.794.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA308"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA308.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA308.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span>paign, with McCain as well as Bush seeming incapable
of understanding the economic crisis, a number of conservatives switched
their support to Obama. Thoughtful conservatives, already disturbed by
the cascade of blunders by the Bush Administration, culminating in its
hesitant and erratic response to the financial crisis that it had failed
to anticipate yet had done much to nurture by its regulatory laxity,
were appalled at the intellectual vacuity of the Republican presidential
campaign, its economic populism, and its preoccupation with abortion and
guns as political issues of
tran<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA308.w.1.0.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>scendent national importance.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA308" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0018.xml_block.331.1.0.box.153.299.907.1608.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA308.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA308.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>The
Republican Party flaunted the anti-intellectual ism of its supporters,
deriding highly educated people who speak in complete sentences as
"elitists"—an attitude that sorted badly with the strong intellectual
tradition of conservatism. It is a selfdefeating strategy of
conservatives to argue that all intellectuals are liberal and therefore
conservatives should think with their guts rather than their brains. The
economic crisis in which the nation finds itself cannot be solved in the
gut.

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA308.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA308.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>This
is not to paper over the failures of liberalism. The nation has
accumulated a substantial history of both liberal and conservative
failures. The<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA309.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_para.332.1.0.box.155.316.907.1612.q.70"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA309"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA309.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA309.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> liberal failures include lack of realism about
human nature, nostalgia for failed social experiments such as
adversarial unionization, underestimation of the social costs of
egalitarian nostrums and of social engineering by judges (the Warren
Court, Roe v. *Wade,* the near abolition of capital punishment), and
underestimation of the social benefits of discipline, of punishment, of
enforcing principles of personal responsibility, and of military force.
The conservative failures include a nostalgia for the social values of
the 1950s or earlier, a strong tendency to deny inconvenient facts (such
a<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA309.w.1.0.0.1" shape="rect"></span>s
the human contribution to global warming), and an overestimation of the
efficiency of unregulated markets, the efficacy of military force, and
the beneficent effects of religiosity in public life. Liberals are wrong
to promote adversarial unionism and conservatives are wrong to
discourage sex education in public schools and promote abstinence as a
substitute for condoms in preventing teenage pregnancy. Liberals are
trapped in fantasies of equality and conservatives in the dogma of
economic libertarianism and often in religious dogmas as well. At
present the conservative failures
<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA309.w.1.0.0.2"
shape="rect"></span>stand out more because the nation has just come to
the end of an unpopular Republican administration —unpopular, as
I</span><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA309.w.1.0.0.3"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA310"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA310.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA310.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA310.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_para.333.1.0.box.154.294.905.219.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"> have noted, even among many conservatives —and
because those failures have been highlighted in a most dramatic way by
the depression that the nation now finds itself in.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA309" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0018.xml_block.332.1.0.box.155.316.907.1612.q.70"
class="gtxt_body">

</div>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA310" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0018.xml_block.333.1.0.box.154.294.911.1614.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA310.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA310.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>I know
it isn't *really* possible to think without preconceptions—I made the
point earlier. As Bayesian decision theory teaches, a rational
decision-maker starts with a prior probability of some uncertain event
(that a credit crunch will turn into a depression, for example) but
adjusts that probability as new evidence comes to his attention. Yet
this means that his prior belief—his preconception— may, depending on
the strength and direction of the evidence, affect his ultimate
decision. That decision will be based on his posterior probability that
the event will occur —the probability that he
arr<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA310.w.1.1.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>ives at by updating his prior probability by
whatever evidence he can find that bears on the matter. Nor do I mean to
deny the value of theory, in particular economic theory, in guiding
policy; economics has provided the framework of my analysis in this
book. But there is a difference between rational preconceptions, based
on theory and experience, and rigid emotional preconceptions, such as
dogmatic libertarianism or egalitarianism or ungrounded hopeful beliefs
such as that<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA311.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_para.334.1.0.box.155.314.908.602.q.70"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA311"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA311.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA311.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> everybody in the world is yearning for democracy
and all democrats are friends of America. Such preconceptions, which can
seize a decision-maker's mind when disagreement among the experts
deprives him of authoritative guidance, tell one more about the
thinker's personality and the limitations of his experience and
imagination than about his competence to make sensible decisions, and
may be impervious to reconsideration in the light of new
evidence.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA311.w.1.0.0.1" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA311.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA311" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0018.xml_block.334.1.0.box.154.314.909.1614.q.70"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA311.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

The spectrum of respectable macroeconomic theorizing runs from left
interventionists to right libertarians. It would be a mistake to commit
to either extreme, because neither can yet be shown to be correct. The
interventionists are prone to underestimate the virtues of markets and
the libertarians to exaggerate them. The latter tendency is more
dangerous at the present juncture. Libertarian economists failed to
grasp the dangers of deregulating the financial markets and
underestimated the risk and depth of the financial crisis. Their
influence was a factor in the government's
a<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA311.w.1.1.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>llowing a self-inflicted capitalist wound to infect
the economy with its first depression in three quarters of a century.

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA311.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA311.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>The
solution is not to banish politics from
eco<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA312.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_para.335.1.0.box.154.297.902.909.q.70"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA312"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA312.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA312.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span>nomic policymaking. Although in principle policies
dedicated to economic efficiency would make everyone better off if
supplemented by transfers from the people who benefit the most from
economic growth to those who benefit the least or not at all, no one
imagines that such transfers would be feasible. There are conflicts
within society that can be resolved only by political competition. Only
politics can adjudicate and mitigate the inevitable conflicts between
groups that have different values and interests. And it is not as if
turning governance over to a cadre of technical
expert<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA312.w.1.0.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>s is a surefire solution to economic or any other
problems of government. Brilliant people screw up all the time.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA312" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0018.xml_block.335.1.0.box.154.297.904.1535.q.70"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA312.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA312.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>The
space for pragmatic, apolitical, nonideological solutions to economic
crises can be enlarged. A hopeful note has been sounded by the Obama
Administration's decision to follow monetarist *and* deficit-spending
prescriptions for curing a depression. Since no one knows which is
better, and since the economic situation appears to be extremely grave,
the prudent course is to try both without worrying which is the more
liberal or the more conservative. We recall that there are three
ri<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA313.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_para.336.1.0.box.154.312.902.298.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA313"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA313.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA313.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span>val deficit-spending approaches and two monetarist
approaches, plus bailouts. Six social experiments (at least) will be
going on at the same time, rather than having one approach be adopted on
theoretical grounds at the outset.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA313.w.1.0.0.1" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA313.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA313" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0018.xml_block.336.1.0.box.152.312.905.1551.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA313.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

It is true that by taking several approaches simultaneously, the
government may make it impossible to determine the value of each
approach, just as in 1937, when the government both raised interest
rates (an anti-monetarist measure, because raising interest rates
reduces the money supply) and curtailed public spending (an
anti-Keynesian measure, because public spending is the Keynesian recipe
for ending a depression). By taking these measures the government
prolonged the depression—and no one can be sure which measure had the
worse effect. At the end of the present
depression<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA313.w.1.1.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>, the theoretical, ideologically salted struggle
between monetarists and deficit spenders will resume if it turns out to
be impossible to separate the effects of the different measures.

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA313.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA313.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>Until
then, let us hope that economists respond to the crisis in the spirit of
pragmatism rather than of ideology. They may not agree on the best way
to cure or at least shorten a depression, but
they<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA314.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_para.337.1.0.box.154.297.906.294.q.70"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA314"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA314.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA314.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> can provide decision-makers with the range of
plausible policies, the spectrum of responsible professional opinion,
the pros and cons of alternative measures, and, not least, an awareness
of the limits of economic understanding.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0018.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA314" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0018.xml_block.337.1.0.box.154.297.906.294.q.70"
class="gtxt_body">

</div>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0018.xml_GBS.PA314.w.1.0.0.1" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<span id="content-0019.xml"></span>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA315"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA315.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA315" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0019.xml_block.338.0.0.box.489.564.232.37.q.90"
class="gtxt_body" style="margin-bottom: 1.5em;">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA315.w.0.0.0" shape="rect"></span>

Conclusion

</div>

<div id="content-0019.xml_block.338.1.0.box.154.819.906.1109.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA315.w.1.0.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA315.w.1.0.0" shape="rect"></span>THIS
IS <span class="smallcaps">NOT </span>a long book, but it has covered a
lot of ground, and a brief recapitulation may be helpful.

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA315.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA315.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>We are
in a depression. (Some economists will not acknowledge a depression
until they see breadlines and Hoovervilles.) It is the product of a
financial crisis that resulted from the confluence of two dangerous
developments: low interest rates in the early 2000s and the deregulation
movement, which began in the 1970s. Low interest rates make borrowing
cheap and make safe saving (as by purchasing certificates of deposit or
other very safe interest-bearing securities) unattractive, so personal
debt rises and the personal savings rate declines. Because houses are
bought mainly with de<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA315.w.1.1.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>bt (a long-term mortgage), low interest rates
encourage the purchase of houses, and because the stock of housing
expands only slowly, an increase in the
demand<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA316.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_para.339.1.0.box.154.294.906.486.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA316"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA316.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA316.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> for houses leads to an increase in their prices.
Low interest rates, by stimulating economic activity (people borrow to
spend), tend to increase stock values, so people tend to buy common
stock in lieu of safe securities. Thus, with low interest rates,
people's savings increasingly take the form of pricey houses and pricey
stocks —and rising prices convince people that houses and stocks are
good investments.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA316.w.1.0.0.1" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA316.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA316" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0019.xml_block.339.1.0.box.154.294.908.1615.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA316.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

The demand for credit is met primarily by financial intermediaries, that
is, by companies —classically, commercial banks —that borrow from one
group of firms or individuals and turn around and lend the borrowed
money to another group. To make a profit the intermediary must pay a
lower interest rate for his borrowed capital than he charges for his
loans. As a practical matter this requires him to offer safety to the
persons he borrows from and to assume risk in the loans he makes. This
is typically done by borrowing short and lending long. A person will
charge a low interest rate if he can
q<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA316.w.1.1.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>uickly get back the money he has lent, because there
is less risk of losing it and it gives him greater liquidity (the
ability to put one's hands on cash when needed). He will charge a higher
interest rate for a long-term loan because it will not be liquid and
because something untoward that
might<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA317.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_para.340.1.0.box.155.315.903.422.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA317"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA317.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA317.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> cause the borrower to default is likelier to occur
over a long period of time than over a short one. Banking thus is
inherently risky; if a bank experiences a lot of defaults on its loans,
its creditors—the persons who have provided it with shortterm capital
—will become anxious and yank their money.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA317" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0019.xml_block.340.1.0.box.155.315.903.1611.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA317.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA317.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>The
inherent riskiness of banking, and the importance of credit in a
commercial society, led to strict regulation of banking, producing a
system in which banks' principal capital consisted of demand deposits
that banks were forbidden to pay interest on. With this cheap source of
capital, banks could make a profit by making short-term rather than
long-term loans, and thus were not at high risk of loss in their loan
portfolios, Federal deposit insurance capped safe banking by making
depositors unworried about such losses.

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA317.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA317.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>The
deregulation movement that began in the 1970s was aimed at the regulated
industries in general, and encompassed banking only because it was
highly regulated. The economists and eventually the politicians who
pressed for deregulation were not sensitive to the fact that
deregulating banking has a macroeconomic significance that deregulating
railroads or trucking or airlines or
telecommu<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA318.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_para.341.1.0.box.153.296.905.1109.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA318"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA318.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA318.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span>nications or oil pipelines does not. The
deregulation of banking proceeded along two paths. Financial
intermediaries that were not banks, such as investment banks, money
market funds, and hedge funds, were increasingly permitted to offer
close substitutes for conventional bank services. An example is a
checkable interest-bearing account in a money market fund —a close
substitute for a checking account in a bank, not quite as safe but quite
safe and paying interest, which checkable bank accounts in a bank did
not. The second path was relaxing the regulatory restrictions on
ban<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA318.w.1.0.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>ks to enable them to compete with the nonbank
financial intermediaries that were crowding them. Increasingly banks
relied on short-term credit other than deposits, and increasingly they
lent long as well as short; their lending thus was increasingly
risky.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA318.w.1.0.0.2" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA318.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA318" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0019.xml_block.341.1.0.box.153.296.906.1612.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA318.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

With the low interest rates of the early 2000s, the banks and other
financial intermediaries found it convenient and profitable to meet the
huge demand for loans for the purchase of housing (and also for the
purchase of commercial real estate, automobiles, and items bought with
credit cards, and for college tuition) by borrowing, and by borrowing
more rather than by raising equity capital, thus
in<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA319.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_para.342.1.0.box.155.314.908.804.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA319"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA319.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA319.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span>creasing the ratio of debt to equity in their
capital structure (that is, increasing their leverage). Deregulation had
made the banking industry (broadly defined, as it should be, to include
the other financial intermediaries, as they were more and more like
banks) more competitive, squeezing profit margins. High leverage enabled
small profit margins to become large ones —as long as the rate of
default on the banks' loans was low. Leverage magnifies profits, but
equally losses, because debts are a fixed obligation, due and owing
regardless of how profitable or unprofitable the banks'
lending<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA319.w.1.0.0.1"
shape="rect"></span> and other investing turn out to be.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA319" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0019.xml_block.342.1.0.box.155.314.908.1613.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA319.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA319.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>The
trends toward easy credit and deregulated and therefore risky lending
were mutually reinforcing. Banks realized this and tried to reduce the
riskiness of their loan portfolios without reducing profit by devices
such as securitized debt (notably securities backed by residential
mortgages) and credit-default swaps (insurance against defaults), which
dispersed risk and therefore reduced the risks borne by a particular
bank. These instruments did reduce risk, but also increased it, making
the net effect on the banking industry's risk unclear; for example,
banks both issued and <span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA319.w.1.1.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>bought credit-default swaps —they were insurers of
defaults as well<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA320.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_para.343.1.0.box.154.293.910.600.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA320"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA320.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA320.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> as being insured against them. The riskiness of
secnritized debt was hard to assess because of the complexity of the
securities, a single one of which might be backed by hundreds or even
thousands of mortgages on houses scattered all over the country.
Elaborate mathematical models ("value of risk" models) were created to
enable banks to try to quantify the risks that their increasingly
complex and risky capital structures and business practices had
created.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA320.w.1.0.0.1" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA320.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA320" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0019.xml_block.343.1.0.box.154.293.910.1613.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA320.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

It is widely believed that the able and often brilliant executives of
the major banks and other financial intermediaries, many of which had
long histories of outstanding success (such as Goldman Sachs), were
actually stupid, and that that is why the banking system collapsed in
September 2008. I do not agree. 1 do not believe that the financial
crisis is the result of stupidity or irrationality, criminality or even
ignorance. I think that even most of the consumers who bought houses
with mortgages they could not "afford" knew what they were
doing—speculating not unreasonably on a
continue<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA320.w.1.1.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>d rise in house prices; for had not Bernanke himself
declared in October 2005, shortly before the housing bubble burst, that
those high prices were *not* a bubble phenomenon? The banks knew they
were<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA321.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_para.344.1.0.box.154.311.905.734.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA321"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA321.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA321.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> taking a lot of risks, and that if they faced a
cascade of defaults they could go broke. Hence the efforts at
diversification and the risk-management models. The banks knew that
because they were heavily invested in the housing industry, if housing
prices fell steeply there would be many defaults. They also knew that
they (and therefore other financial intermediaries as well) were
entangled with each other —a bank might originate a mortgage-backed
security and sell a piece of it to another bank and at the same time buy
a piece of a similar security issued by that other bank.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA321" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0019.xml_block.344.1.0.box.153.311.909.1612.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA321.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA321.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>Though
I am not certain of this, I believe that the senior executives of the
major banks were aware that there might be at least a small probability
of bankruptcy as a result of the risks they were taking. I think most of
them knew or suspected that if housing prices dropped more than 20
percent on average many banks might go broke, especially because such a
big drop in housing prices would herald a recession (regardless of the
direct impact on the banking industry), and a recession would increase
the default rate even of bank loans unrelated to real estate; it would
also further i<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA321.w.1.1.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>ncrease mortgage defaults as people lost their jobs.

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA321.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA321.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>It is
important not to confuse making
mistakes<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA322.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_para.345.1.0.box.154.292.905.798.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA322"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA322.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA322.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> with being stupid. In an op-ed column on February
2, 2009, revealingly entitled "Bailouts for Bunglers," Paul Krugman
opposes the claim that the government should not take over banks because
it doesn't know how to run them with the argument that the "bunglers"
who ran the banks that collapsed last fall don't know how to run banks
either. But they do; and if their mistakes should bar them from being
permitted to continue to run banks, then the mistakes that Bcrnanke and
Summers and Geithner have made in the regulation of financial
intermediation should bar them from managing the
<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA322.w.1.0.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>nation's economic policy.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA322.w.1.0.0.2" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA322.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA322" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0019.xml_block.345.1.0.box.154.292.908.1615.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA322.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

Because a great many banks were heavily invested in financing
residential real estate, and because banks were financially entangled
with one another, if one bank faced a 1 percent probability of failure,
then a great many did. Which meant that if the 1 percent probability
materialized, so many banks would be broke or nearly broke that the
entire system of credit would freeze. And so it happened in September
2008. But no single bank, in the highly competitive
financial-intermediation industry, could justify to its shareholders
reducing its risk-taking (for example by reducing its
le<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA322.w.1.1.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>verage), and therefore their return on equity,
merely because the
risks<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA323.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_para.346.1.0.box.153.311.908.1165.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA323"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA323.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA323.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> that it and its competitors were taking might
precipitate a financial crisis that could in turn usher in a depression,
just as a wave of bank insolvencies caused by the bursting of a
credit-fed stock bubble had ushered in the Great Depression of the 1930s
and a wave of bank insolvencies caused by the bursting of a real estate
bubble had ushered in Japan's "lost decade" of the 1990s. There would be
only one effect of the bank's altruism —of its willingness to sacrifice
profits enabled by taking a slight risk of bankruptcy that most
financial executives would think tolerable, as the
risk<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA323.w.1.0.0.1" shape="rect"></span>
would be unlikely to materialize for a number of years during which they
would be making huge amounts of money: the bank would lose out in
competition with its daring competitors. And they *would* be daring,
because financial intermediation, being an inherently risky business
activity, attracts people who are comfortable with risk.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA323" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0019.xml_block.346.1.0.box.152.311.909.1615.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA323.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA323.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>There
was a race to the bottom —or the top, depending on one's perspective.
The most daring, aggressive players in the financial sandbox would ramp
up the riskiness of their lending or other investing, and this would
increase their returns, at least in the short run. Their timid
competitors would be forced to match the daring ones'
strategy<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA324.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_para.347.1.0.box.154.292.903.173.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA324"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA324.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA324.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> or drop out of the competition. I am told that some
bank officials asked the federal regulatory agencies to rein in their
competitors, but to no avail.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA324.w.1.0.0.1" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA324.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA324" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0019.xml_block.347.1.0.box.153.292.911.1615.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA324.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

It is no more realistic to expect banks to internalize the remote risk
of economy-wide catastrophe that their lawful pursuit of profit creates
than to expect people who fear loss of job and income in a depression to
spend, not save, for the greater good, though if enough people save
enough money in times of economic trouble, the result could be a
disastrous deflation.

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA324.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA324.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>What
is tolerable risk for a company—that it will very probably go broke
sometime during the century— is not for the nation. The risk to the
nation is not the bankruptcy of a single major bank but the collapse of
the banking industry, precipitating a financial crisis that can bring on
a depression — that has done so. This is all the more likely to happen
because one of the consequences of easy credit is a high level of
personal debt. When a collapse of the banking industry freezes credit,
economic output falls because of the disruption of the normal course of
business, which depends on
c<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA324.w.1.2.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>redit. With output dropping, layoffs begin. People
with heavy debt who lose or fear losing their jobs reduce their
spending, which causes a further drop in output.
If<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA325.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_para.348.1.0.box.153.311.905.864.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA325"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA325.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA325.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> output falls sharply, as it has been doing in
recent months and seems set to continue doing, sellers will reduce their
prices in order to retain customers and clear inventory. If the price
drop is substantial and creates expectations of further and even deeper
price cuts, people may begin hoarding cash, hoping to be able to buy
more goods and services in the future because the purchasing power of
cash will grow. Hoarding causes further drops in spending and also in
borrowing —no one wants to borrow who thinks he'll be repaying the loan
in dollars that are worth more because
p<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA325.w.1.0.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>rices will be lower—and then we are in a deflation,
and the downward spiral gathers momentum.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA325" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0019.xml_block.348.1.0.box.152.311.906.1615.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA325.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA325.w.1.1.0"
shape="rect"></span>Businessmen can no more afford to consider the
effect of their decisions on the economy as a whole than consumers can.
Consumers spend a smaller part of their incomes in a depression because
they want to build up their precautionary savings —but by reducing their
spending they make the depression worse. (In the topsy-turvy world of
depression economics, it is possible to argue almost with a straight
face that people should save when they are flush and spend when they are
broke—and that inventory that builds up in a depression should be
destroyed to stimulate new production.)
Behavior<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA326.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_para.349.1.0.box.154.292.903.110.q.70"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA326"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA326.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA326.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> that generates large external costs provides an apt
occasion for government regulation.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA326.w.1.0.0.1" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA326.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA326" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0019.xml_block.349.1.0.box.153.292.909.1615.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA326.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

We are in a depression and struggling to get out of it. But what has
brought us to this pass? Low interest rates do not appear from nowhere,
nor movements to deregulate the financial sector. The low interest rates
of the early 2000s were a product of a deliberate decision by the
Federal Reserve. The deregulation movement was the response to justified
criticism of common-carrier and public-utility regulation. But the
economists who pushed deregulation, as I said, were not macroeconomists
sensitive to the role of banking regulation in preventing risk-taking
that could bring on a
depr<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA326.w.1.1.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>ession, while the macroeconomists viewed low
interest rates with complacence because they thought that a depression
could easily be prevented, precisely by keeping interest rates low and
thus stimulating economic activity. The danger they perceived in low
interest rates was inflation, and when the consumer price index stayed
down during the early 2000s despite the low interest rates prevailing in
that period they thought they had found the Holy Grail. Actually the low
interest rates had produced inflationary pressures, but pressures that
pushed up the price of housing and to a lesser extent of
common<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA327.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_para.350.1.0.box.154.309.905.359.q.70"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA327"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA327.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA327.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> stocks rather than the price of items that dominate
the consumer price index. Depressions were thought a thing of the past,
and Robert LAicas pronounced the end of depression economics and urged
macroeconomists to shift their attention to issues of economic
growth.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA327" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0019.xml_block.350.1.0.box.152.309.909.1604.q.60"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA327.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA327.w.1.1.0"
shape="rect"></span>Economists were strongly influenced by Milton
Friedman's argument that the Great Depression had been the result of the
Federal Reserve's shrinking the money supply when prices and output fell
in the wake of the stock market crash of October 1929 —that had the Fed
kept up the money supply the nation would have experienced nothing worse
than a recession. Insufficient weight was given to the fact that the
Federal Reserve's creation of money is mediated by the banking industry.
The Fed in effect licenses the banks to create money by making loans,
and if they are afraid to lend (more
preci<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA327.w.1.1.0.1"
shape="rect"></span>sely, if they set stringent criteria for lending,
because the risk of default is so great) and if overindebted people are
afraid to borrow lest they have to repay their loans in more valuable
dollars, the money supply may shrink despite the Fed's efforts, causing
deflation to accelerate as prices fall and firms and individuals hoard
cash. At that point, the efforts that the Federal Reserve would have
to<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA328.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_para.351.1.0.box.155.299.900.296.q.70"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA328"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA328.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA328.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> exert in order to arrest the deflationary spiral
might plant the seeds of a terrible post-depression inflation. And in
anticipation of the inflation, interest rates might rise rather than
fall, choking off recovery from the depression.</span>

</div>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA328.w.1.0.0.1" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div style="display:none;">

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA328.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

</div>

<div class="flow" style="">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA328" class="page"
shape="rect"></span>

<div id="content-0019.xml_block.351.1.0.box.154.299.903.1609.q.70"
class="gtxt_body">

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA328.w.1.1.0" shape="rect"></span>

The failure of the economics profession to have grasped the dangers that
have now produced the first U.S. depression since the 1930s is
excusable. Ideology has played a role in this professional blindness,
but that is unavoidable because of the difficulty of empirically testing
rival theories of depression and the political significance of
depressions and responses to them. The fact that finance and
macroeconomics have become separate fields with some difficulties of
intercommunication may have been the inevitable result of the relentless
pressure for ever-greater specialization
in<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA328.w.1.1.0.1" shape="rect"></span>
academic disciplines. Even the failure of officials and of most academic
economists to heed the abundant warning signs of the coming crash is, if
not excusable, at least readily understandable; Cassandras rarely
receive a fair hearing, and for reasons that only in hindsight can be
seen to be mistaken.

<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA328.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>

<span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA328.w.1.2.0" shape="rect"></span>What
is inexcusable is the failure of the Federal Reserve and other economic
agencies within the federal government to have prepared
contingency<span id="content-0019.xml_ORIG-GBS.PA329.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_para.352.1.0.box.152.310.906.1613.q.60"
class="gtxt_body"><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA329"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA329.w.0.0.0"
shape="rect"></span><span id="content-0019.xml_GBS.PA329.w.1.0.0"
shape="rect"></span> plans for the possibility, remote as it seemed,
that a crumbling of the banking industry would set the stage for a
depression. When the financial crisis hit in mid-September 2008, the
government was unprepared and responded with a series of improvisations
that did avert the most catastrophic imaginable consequences of the
